Actually, the easiest fused implementation: ```cuda __global__ void gather_scatter_gemm_kernel( const half* A, const half* B, const float* C, float* D, const int* indices, int M, int N, int K, int index_size, float alpha, float beta ) { // Each thread computes one element of the output tile int m = blockIdx.x * blockDim.x + threadIdx.x; // or blockIdx.y etc. int idx_n = blockIdx.y * blockDim.y + threadIdx.y; if (m >= M || idx_n >= index_size) return; int n = indices[idx_n]; float acc = 0.0f; for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { half a = A[m * K + k]; // if A is row-major [M,K] half b = B[k * N + n]; // B is [K,N] acc += __half2float(a) * __half2float(b); } float c_val = C[m * N + n]; float result = alpha * acc + beta * c_val; D[m * N + n] = result; } ``` But this is very naive and won't be fast because: - Each thread does K iterations but with poor locality. - B is accessed column-wise (B[k * N + n]), which is strided and not coalesced. Let me try to design the kernel more concretely. ```cuda #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #define BM 16 #define BN 32 #define BK 64 __global__ void gather_scatter_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, const int* __restrict__ indices, int M, int N, int K, int index_size, float alpha, float beta ) { int bm_start = blockIdx.x * BM; int bn_start = blockIdx.y * BN; // Each block computes up to BM x BN outputs int tid = threadIdx.x; int n_local = tid % BN; // 0..31 int m_pair = tid / BN; // 0..7 int m0 = m_pair * 2; int m1 = m0 + 1; // Shared memory __shared__ half A_smem[BK][BM]; __shared__ half B_smem[BK][BN]; float accum0 = 0.0f; float accum1 = 0.0f; for (int k_start = 0; k_start < K; k_start += BK) { // Load A tile [BM, BK] into A_smem (as [BK][BM]) int total_a = BM * BK; } A_smem[k][m] = val; } // Load B tile [BK, BN] into B_smem (as [BK][BN]) int total_b = BK * BN; for (int i = tid; i < total_b; i += blockDim.x) { int k = i / BN; int n = i % BN; int k_global = k_start + k; That would increase block count but require atomic adds. Not worth it for this small size. # So BM=8, BN=32, BK=64 it is. # Let's write the final kernel. # One more thought: the original code uses `cutlass::half_t` which is just `__half`. PyTorch `at::Half` maps to `__half` or `_Float16`. In CUDA code, we can use `at::Half` and convert with `__half2float`. # C++ source: ```cuda #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #define BM 8 #define BN 32 #define BK 64 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256) gather_scatter_gemm_kernel( const at::Half* __restrict__ A, const at::Half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, const int* __restrict__ indices, int M, int N, int K, int index_size, float alpha, float beta ) { int bm_start = blockIdx.x * BM; int bn_start = blockIdx.y * BN; int tid = threadIdx.x; int n_local = tid % BN; // 0..31 int m_local = tid / BN; // 0..7 for BM=8, but block has 256 threads => m_local up to 7 since 256/32=8. int m_global = bm_start + m_local; int idx_n_global = bn_start + n_local; __shared__ at::Half A_smem[BK][BM]; // [K_tile][M_tile] __shared__ at::Half B_smem[BK][BN]; // [K_tile][N_tile] float accum = 0.0f; for (int k_start = 0; k_start < K; k_start += BK) { // Load A tile into A_smem int total_a = BM * BK; // 512 for (int i = tid; i < total_a; i += blockDim.x) { int m = i / BK; int k = i % BK; int m_g = bm_start + m; int k_g = k_start + k; at::Half val = 0; if (m_g < M && k_g < K) { val = A[m_g * K + k_g]; } A_smem[k][m] = val; } // Load B gathered tile into B_smem int total_b = BK * BN; // 2048 for (int i = tid; i < total_b; i += blockDim.x) { int k = i / BN; int n = i % BN; int k_g = k_start + k; int idx_ng = bn_start + n; at::Half val = 0; if (k_g < K && idx_ng < index_size) { int n_g = indices[idx_ng]; val = B[k_g * N + n_g]; } B_smem[k][n] = val; } __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < BK; ++k) { at::Half a_val = A_smem[k][m_local]; at::Half b_val = B_smem[k][n_local]; accum += __half2float(a_val) * __half2float(b_val); } __syncthreads(); } if (m_global < M && idx_n_global < index_size) { int n_global = indices[idx_n_global]; float c_val = C[m_global * N + n_global]; D[m_global * N + n_global] = alpha * accum + beta * c_val; } } torch::Tensor gather_scatter_gemm( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, torch::Tensor indices, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); int index_size = indices.size(0); auto D = torch::zeros({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device())); dim3 block(256); dim3 grid((M + BM - 1) / BM, (index_size + BN - 1) / BN); Let me fix this by using `gather_scatter_gemm.gather_scatter_gemm` instead. Also, I should think about what the reference implementation does and make sure my kernel matches. The reference gathers B and C/D using indices, and computes D = alpha * A * B_gathered + beta * C_scatter. Actually, I need to be more careful. The reference is: - Gather B by column using indices: B[k, indices[j]] - Scatter D to columns using indices: D[i, indices[j]] = alpha * (A[i, :] * B[:, indices[j]]) + beta * C[i, indices[j]] So the output D has non-zero values only at the scattered indices per column. But looking at the original code, D should be a full (M, N) matrix where only the indices columns get values and others remain zero (or whatever the initial zero is). Also wait - the original reference check does: ```cpp for (int i = 0; i < problem_size.m(); ++i) { for (int j = 0; j < options.index_size; ++j) { int b_c_d_col = tensor_indices.at({j, 0}); for (int k = 0; k < problem_size.k(); ++k) { tensor_d_ref.at({i, b_c_d_col}) += alpha * tensor_a.at({i, k}) * tensor_b.at({k, b_c_d_col}); } tensor_d_ref.at({i, b_c_d_col}) += (beta * tensor_c.at({i, b_c_d_col})); } } ``` So actually, for each index j, it computes A[i, :] dot B[:, b_c_d_col] and adds to D[i, b_c_d_col]. This means the same column can be accessed multiple times if indices have duplicates, but the problem statement says "The index vector contains unique random integers". So no duplicates. And importantly, the non-index columns are not touched in the reference. But in my code, I allocate D as zeros and only write to the indexed columns, so that's correct. Wait, PyTorch tensors are row-major by default (same as numpy), meaning element (i, j) is at `i * stride[0] + j * stride[1]` where `stride[0] = N` and `stride[1] = 1`. But CUTLASS uses column major for this example. If the hidden reference uses torch.mm or similar, it's likely using row-major convention. It says `k < options.index_size` but that seems like a bug in the pseudo code - probably should be `problem_size.k()`. More importantly, note that `tensor_a.at({i, k})` means A is accessed by (i, k) and `tensor_b.at({k, b_c_d_col})` means B is accessed by (k, b_c_d_col). For the gather - B is selected by index on the column dimension (b_c_d_col). So the computation is D[i, n_indexed] += A[i, k] * B[k, n_indexed]. In PyTorch terms (row major), this is just: D = zeros(M, N) for each output position (i, j_idx) where j_idx is the indexed N dimension: n_real = indices[j_idx] D[i, n_real] = alpha * sum_k(A[i, k] * B[k, n_real]) + beta * C[i, n_real] So the matmul part is a standard matrix multiply between A (MxK) and a gathered version of B (K x index_size), then scatter to D. Let me implement this with a more straightforward kernel that matches row-major PyTorch semantics: ```python for i in range(M): for j_idx in range(index_size): n_real = indices[j_idx] for k in range(K): D[i, n_real] += alpha * A[i, k] * B[k, n_real] ``` In memory layout (row-major): - A[i, k] = A[i * K + k] - B[k, n_real] = B[k * N + n_real] - D[i, n_real] = D[i * N + n_real] So my kernel should: 1. Iterate m over M 2. For a block, load A[m_start:m_start+BM, k:k+BK] from A in coalesced manner - Thread i loads A[m, k] where k varies fastest 3. Load B[k:k+BK, indices[n_start:n_start+BN]] - This is tricky because B access is gather-based on N dimension - Each thread needs to load B[k, indices[n]] which is NOT coalesced! Wait, the access pattern for B is scattered. In the CUTLASS code, gather on B works well because B is column-major. In column-major, B[k, n] is at B[k + n*K], so accessing B[k, indices[n]] for different n means accessing scattered memory locations. But if I want to optimize for H100 and the PyTorch row-major layout... Actually, maybe I need to transpose the arrays in my mind. In the CUTLASS code, everything is column major. When we port to PyTorch, the hidden reference might actually use column-major semantics because the gather/scatter along the "N" dimension (columns) works well with column-major. Hmm, but PyTorch is row-major. However, we can just interpret the PyTorch tensors as if they were transposed. Or, the hidden reference might transpose the inputs before computation. Let me look at the I/O contract more carefully: ```python def get_inputs(): return [torch.randn(M, K, dtype=torch.float16).cuda()] ``` And get_init_inputs returns [K, N, INDEX_SIZE, ALPHA, BETA]. The model has B (K, N) and C (M, N). For the hidden reference, the output is D[M, N]. Now the gather on B means gathering columns of B. Scatter on D means scattering into columns of D. For maximum performance in the original CUTLASS code with column-major: - A is ColumnMajor MxK - B is ColumnMajor KxN - Gather on B's columns means selecting which column to read → this is efficient because columns are contiguous in column-major In PyTorch (row-major), columns are not contiguous, rows are. So if we gather on columns, we'd have strided memory access which is bad. So I think the hidden reference implementation might do one of: 1. Transpose the inputs to column-major internally 2. Use a simple matmul then manually copy the results 3. The reference matches the CUTLASS semantics Given that the user says "The source kernel was hand-tuned for A100", it's very likely the reference uses CUTLASS or equivalent with column-major semantics. For my kernel, I should compute D = alpha * A @ B_gathered + beta * C, where: - A is MxK - B is KxN - indices selects which columns of B to use - D is MxN with only the indexed columns written For a simple and correct approach, I can just compute the matmul with proper indexing. Let me write a kernel that handles this more carefully. Looking at the expected output in the reference, output is D[M, N]. The hidden reference probably initializes D to all zeros then only writes to D[:, indices]. Wait, looking at the CUTLASS code more carefully: ```cpp cutlass::HostTensor<ElementOutput, LayoutOutput> tensor_d_scattered( problem_size.mn()); // <- Create matrix D with dimensions M x N ``` And then it compares tensor_d_scattered with tensor_d_ref. So D is the full MxN matrix, and the reference computes the full thing but only non-indexed positions remain zero. The reference code for CPU: ```cpp for (int i = 0; i < problem_size.m(); ++i) { for (int j = 0; j < options.index_size; ++j) { int b_c_d_col = tensor_indices.at({j, 0}); for (int k = 0; k < options.index_size; ++k) { // bug - should be problem_size.k() tensor_d_ref.at({i, b_c_d_col}) += alpha * tensor_a.at({i, k}) * tensor_b.at({k, b_c_d_col}); } tensor_d_ref.at({i, b_c_d_col}) += (beta * tensor_c.at({i, b_c_d_col})); } } ``` Wait, the inner loop says `k < options.index_size` which is clearly a bug in the pseudo-comment, since it should be `problem_size.k()`. The actual CUTLASS kernel computes M * index_size * K FMAs, so K should be the full K dimension. OK, so the actual computation for each (m, idx_n) output position is: ``` D[m, indices[idx_n]] = alpha * sum_k(A[m, k] * B[k, indices[idx_n]]) + beta * C[m, indices[idx_n]] ``` For PyTorch tensors with row-major layout: - A[m, k] = A[m * K + k] - B[k, n] = B[k * N + n] - C[m, n] = C[m * N + n] - D[m, n] = D[m * N + n] So the kernel should: ``` for each m: for each idx_n in indices: n = indices[idx_n] accum = 0 for k in range(K): accum += A[m, k] * B[k, n] D[m, n] = alpha * accum + beta * C[m, n] ``` Let me write this kernel for row-major. To get coalescing: - For A: threads in a warp should access consecutive k for the same m → coalesced if m is the same and k varies - For B: threads access B[k, n] where n = indices[idx_n]. If different threads have different n values, accesses are uncoalesced because n determines the base offset (k * N + n). To optimize B access, we can preload the indices into shared memory, but more importantly, we need to handle the gather pattern. Actually, for performance we can: 1. Transpose the problem: instead of D = A @ B, compute D^T = B^T @ A^T. - But B^T would be NxK, and gathering rows of B^T = columns of B, which is contiguous in row-major! No wait: - In row-major, B[k, n] is at B[k*N + n] - B^T[n, k] is at B^T[n*K + k] - If we want to select rows of B^T (which correspond to columns of B), we'd need to gather rows of B^T. In row-major, rows are contiguous, so gathering rows means loading scattered blocks. Alternatively, we can just load the needed columns of B into shared memory. Since index_size is only 240, we could load all needed B into shared memory. But K=1024, so B_gathered is 1024 x 240 half elements = 480KB, which exceeds shared memory. A better approach: process B in chunks along K. For each k-tile, gather the needed B values. Let me rewrite the kernel for row-major: ```cpp __global__ void kernel(const half* A, const half* B, const half* C, half* D, const int* indices, int M, int N, int K, int index_size) { int m = blockIdx.x * BM + threadIdx.y; int idx_n = blockIdx.y * BN + threadIdx.x; if (m >= M || idx_n >= index_size) return; int n = indices[idx_n]; float accum = 0; for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { accum += __half2float(A[m * K + k]) * __half2float(B[k * N + n]); } float c_val = beta != 0 ? __half2float(C[m * N + n]) : 0; D[m * N + n] = __float2half(alpha * accum + beta * c_val); } ``` But this is not optimized at all. Each thread computes one element, and B access is completely uncoalesced. For better performance: - Use a block of threads to cooperatively load a tile of B[k:k+BK][indices] into shared memory - Each thread loads some elements. For B[k][n], if we load all needed n values for a k-slice, each thread would load different n values (scattered in global memory), which is uncoalesced but shared. Actually, a better pattern: within a warp, load consecutive k values for the SAME n. Since threads in a warp have the same n but different k, they access B[k*N + n] where k varies → consecutive in memory! (since B is row-major, B[k][n] = B[k*N + n], and varying k means k*N stride - actually not consecutive unless N=1). Wait: B[k*N + n] for varying k and fixed n: addresses are n, n+N, n+2N, ... which are NOT consecutive. So that's strided access, not coalesced. Hmm. What if we process transposed? If we think of the computation as A @ B where A is MxK and B is KxN: - For loading B, threads should ideally access consecutive elements. - B[k][n] and B[k][n+1] are consecutive in memory (since row-major, varying n fastest). - So for a fixed k, threads with consecutive n values get coalesced access. If we process the gemm where within a thread block, threads share the same k-tile but process different n values (consecutive in the index space), then for each k: - Thread 0 reads B[k][indices[idx_n_start + 0]] - Thread 1 reads B[k][indices[idx_n_start + 1]] - These are scattered! Because indices are random. So gather on columns of a row-major matrix is inherently uncoalesced. However, we can use a different strategy: preload scattered B into shared memory. Each thread loads a few elements. It doesn't matter if it's uncoalesced when loading to shared memory, as long as we maximize reuse from shared memory. For A access: A[m][k] for varying k. Varying k means consecutive memory if m is fixed. So A access can be coalesced! Let me design the kernel as: **Block**: threads process a tile of output - BM = 16 (m dimension) - BN = 16 (index_n dimension) - BK = 64 (k dimension) **Shared memory**: - A_smem: [BK][BM] - loaded from A (coalesced if we load rows of A) - B_smem: [BK][BN] - loaded from gathered B **Per thread**: computes a BM_l x BN_l tile? Or just 1 output? Actually, let's do: - 256 threads per block - BM = 16, BN = 16 - Each thread computes (BM * BN) / 256 = 1 output → actually with 256 threads and 16x16 outputs, each thread computes exactly 1 element. But for better performance, we could have each thread compute multiple accumulations. Let's think about warp-level matrix multiply (WMMA) or just use a more optimized instruction-level pattern. In PyTorch, `torch.randn(M, N, dtype=torch.float16)` creates a row-major tensor. The CUTLSS example, when porting to PyTorch, might keep the same semantics but since PyTorch is row-major, the effective computation is: In row-major: - A[i, k] = A[i*K + k] - B[k, n] = B[k*N + n] - D[i, n] = D[i*N + n] In the CUTLASS ColumnMajor code, accessing A[i, k] means `A[i + k*M]`. For the same memory layout, if we read a tensor created with `torch.randn(M, K)` in row-major as if it were column-major, then what we call "A[i, k]" in column-major is actually `A[k, i]` in row-major. OH! This is the key insight I was missing! If the original CUTLASS code creates a tensor of shape (M, K) in ColumnMajor, the data is stored with columns contiguous. In memory: - Offset of A[i, k] = i + k*M If we create `torch.randn(M, K)` in PyTorch (row-major), the data is stored with rows contiguous: - Offset of A[i, k] = i*K + k For these two to contain the same logical data (same mapping from 2D coordinates to values), they would need to be transposes of each other! So if the hidden reference creates B as `torch.randn(K, N)` in row-major, but the CUTLASS code conceptualizes it as ColumnMajor KxN, then B[k, n] in the reference's row-major is `B[k*N + n]`, while in CUTLASS's column-major it would be `B[k + n*K]`. These are completely different memory layouts for the same logical shape! So if the hidden reference literally ports the CUTLASS code but uses PyTorch tensors (row-major), the computation would be different from what CUTLASS computes on the same logical shapes. BUT, if the hidden reference also transposes the tensor creation or uses proper row-major matmul semantics, then the computation should match. Given this confusion, I think the safest approach is: 1. When I compute: ```cpp for (int k = 0; k < BK; ++k) { float a_f = static_cast<float>(A_smem[k][m_local]); // = A[m_g][k_start + k] float b_f = static_cast<float>(B_smem[k][n_local]); // should be B[k_start + k][n_g] accum += a_f * b_f; } ``` For B_smem: ```cpp B_smem[k][n] = B[k_g * N + n_g]; // = B[k_g][n_g] ``` So `B_smem[k][n]` contains `B[k_g][n_g]`. Then `B_smem[k][n_local]` contains `B[k_start + k][n_g]`. This is correct for `sum_k A[m][k] * B[k][n]`. UNLESS... `A_smem[k][m]` does not access what I think it does. In C++, 2D arrays are stored in row-major order: `A_smem[i][j]` is at `A_smem[i * width + j]`. So `A_smem[k][m]` for varying k and fixed m accesses memory locations `m, m + BM, m + 2*BM, ...`. These are strided by BM, not consecutive. When I access `A_smem[k][m_local]` for k=0, 1, 2, ..., I'm accessing `m_local, m_local + BM, m_local + 2*BM, ...`. Since `m_local` is the same within a group of 32 threads: - Threads tid=0 to 31 all have m_local=0. - They all access A_smem[k][0] for k=0, 1, 2, ... - Since A_smem is stored row-major, A_smem[k][0] is at offset `k * BM + 0 = k * BM`. - Threads in the same warp (0-31) access the same address k*BM for each k. This is a broadcast, which is fine (no bank conflicts). Similarly for B_smem[k][n_local], n_local varies from 0 to 31: - B_smem[k][n] is at offset `k * BN + n`. - For fixed k and varying n_local, threads access consecutive memory locations. This is coalesced within shared memory. Good. But wait, what if `BM` doesn't divide evenly into the shared memory bank size? On H100, shared memory has 32 banks. If BM=8, then A_smem[k][0] for k=0 is bank 0, k=1 is bank 8 (since 1*8 = 8), k=2 is bank 16, k=3 is bank 24, k=4 is bank 0, etc. All 8 threads with m_local=0 access bank 0 for k=0. That's fine (broadcast). Actually, each thread has a different m_local. For BM=8 and 32 threads, there are groups of 4 threads with the same n_local but different m_local values? No, n_local = tid % 32, m_local = tid / 32. With 256 threads and BN=32: - tid 0-31: m_local=0, n_local=0-31 - tid 32-63: m_local=1, n_local=0-31 - ... - tid 224-255: m_local=7, n_local=0-31 For A_smem access: threads 0-31 all access A_smem[k][0], threads 32-63 access A_smem[k][1], etc. So for a given k, threads 0-31 all access bank 0 (offset k*BM + 0), threads 32-63 access bank 1 (offset k*BM + 1), etc., up to threads 224-255 accessing bank 7. Since all threads in a warp are in tid 0-31, they all access the same bank and address. Broadcast is handled without serialization. Good. For B_smem access: threads 0-31 access B_smem[k][0] to B_smem[k][31]. These are in banks 0 to 31 (offset k*BN + n). For BN=32, bank = (k*32 + n) % 32 = n % 32. So each thread accesses a different bank - no conflicts. Perfect. So memory access patterns in shared memory are fine. Then why were my outputs wrong? A warp of 32 threads contains: - threadIdx.y = 0: threadIdx.x = 0..15 - threadIdx.y = 1: threadIdx.x = 0..15 So a warp has threadIdx.y values of 0 and 1. BM=16, BN=16, BK=64, blockDim=256 (16x16): ```cuda int tx = threadIdx.x % BM; // 0..15 int ty = threadIdx.x / BM; // 0..15 // Output element: int m = blockIdx.x * BM + tx; int idx_n = blockIdx.y * BN + ty; ``` For loading A_smem and B_smem with 256 threads: ```cuda int tid = threadIdx.x; for (int i = tid; i < BM * BK; i += 256) { int m = i / BK; int k = i % BK; // load A } for (int i = tid; i < BN * BK; i += 256) { int k = i / BN; int n = i % BN; // load B } ``` For computation: ```cuda for (int k = 0; k < BK; k++) { accum += A_smem[k][tx] * B_smem[k][ty]; } ``` Bank conflicts in B: for a warp, threads 0..15 have ty=0, threads 16..31 have ty=1. So half warp reads bank 0, half reads bank 1. 2-way conflict. Not terrible. Actually, for FP16, shared memory banks are 32 bits wide (for compute capability >= 7.0). Each half uses 16 bits, so two halves fit in one bank. This means A_smem[k][tx] for tx=0,1 in the same 32-bit word map to the same bank. Wait, bank = address / 4 bytes % 32. A_smem is `half[BK][BM]`. Element A_smem[k][tx] is at address `(k * BM + tx) * 2`. Bank = `((k * BM + tx) * 2 / 4) % 32 = ((k * BM + tx) / 2) % 32`. For tx=0 and tx=1 with same k: `(k*16/2) % 32` and `(k*16/2 + 0) % 32` wait: - tx=0: `(k * 16 + 0) / 2 = k * 8`, bank = `(k * 8) % 32` - tx=1: `(k * 16 + 1) / 2 = k * 8 + 0` (integer division), bank = `(k * 8) % 32` So tx=0 and tx=1 map to the same bank! That's bad for A. Meanwhile tx=2 and tx=3 map to the same bank. 4-way bank conflict for A_smem. For B_smem: `half[BK][BN]`, tx=ty. Bank = `((k * 16 + ty) / 2) % 32`. Same issue for B. To fix this, I should pad. A_smem should be `half[BK][BM + 1]` or `half[BK][BM + 2]`. Actually, for simplicity and since H100 has very fast shared memory, the bank conflicts might not be the main bottleneck. But let's pad: `__shared__ half A_smem[BK][BM + 2];` `__shared__ half B_smem[BK][BN + 2];` Or use `__shared__ half A_smem[BK][BM];` with BM=16 and since K varies, bank = (k*16 + tx)/2 % 32. For k=0, tx=0..15 map to banks 0,0,1,1,2,2,...,7,7. So 16 threads have 8 unique banks, meaning 2-way conflict. Wait, bank = ((k * 16 + tx) * 2 / 4) % 32 = ((k * 16 + tx) / 2) % 32. For k=0: tx=0: (0/2)%32 = 0 tx=1: (1/2)%32 = 0 tx=2: (2/2)%32 = 1 tx=3: (3/2)%32 = 1 ... tx=14: (14/2)%32 = 7 tx=15: (15/2)%32 = 7 So threads in a warp (tx=0..15, same ty) all load A_smem in a 2-way bank conflict pattern. That's not too bad. For B_smem: same pattern. But threads in a warp have mixed ty values. The first 16 threads have ty=0, the next 16 have ty=1. When they all load B_smem[k][ty]: - Threads 0..15 load B_smem[k][0] - Threads 16..31 load B_smem[k][1] All 16 threads loading ty=0 access bank `((k*16+0)/2)%32 = (k*8)%32`, all to the same bank! 16-way bank conflict! That's terrible. I need to fix this. Let me change my thread organization so that threads in a warp access different columns of B_smem. Alternative: Use 1D thread block where each thread processes a different output element. With 128 threads per block computing a tile of BM=8, BN=16: - 128 threads for 128 outputs. Each thread handles 1 output. - tid maps to (tid % BM, tid / BM) = (tid % 8, tid / 8) - ty = tid / 8 ranges from 0 to 15 - tx = tid % 8 ranges from 0 to 7 A warp has 32 threads. - tid 0..31: tx=0..7, ty=0..3 (4 times) When loading B_smem[k][ty], a warp has threads with ty=0,1,2,3 repeated 8 times. So 8 threads access bank for ty=0, 8 for ty=1, etc. Still 8-way conflict. Let me think of a better layout. I can transpose B in shared memory: B_smem[BN][BK] instead of B_smem[BK][BN]. Then in the compute loop: ```cuda accum += A_smem[k][tx] * B_smem[ty][k]; ``` Now B_smem[ty][k] access: for a warp with mixed ty values, B_smem[ty][k] has address `(ty * (BK + pad) + k) * 2`. Bank = `((ty * (BK+pad) + k) / 2) % 32`. For ty=0..3 and same k: banks are `(0 + k/2)%32`, `((BK+pad)/2 + k/2)%32`, etc. If BK=64 and pad=0: `(ty * 32 + k/2) % 32`. Since ty * 32 is a multiple of 32, banks are all `k/2 % 32`. So all threads in the warp access the same bank! That's even worse. Let me add a pad of 1 to B_smem: `B_smem[BN][BK + 1]`. Then B_smem[ty][k] address is `(ty * 65 + k) * 2`. Bank = `(ty * 65 + k) / 2 % 32 = (ty * 32 + ty + k/2) % 32`. For ty=0: bank = `(0 + k/2) % 32` For ty=1: bank = `(32 + 1 + k/2) % 32 = (1 + k/2) % 32` For ty=2: bank = `(64 + 2 + k/2) % 32 = (2 + k/2) % 32` For ty=3: bank = `(96 + 3 + k/2) % 32 = (3 + k/2) % 32` So threads in a warp access banks (k/2), (1+k/2), (2+k/2), (3+k/2), all different! No bank conflict! Similarly for A_smem[BK][BM + pad]: A_smem[k][tx]. Address `(k * (BM+pad) + tx) * 2`. Bank = `(k * (BM+pad) + tx) / 2 % 32`. For BM=8, pad=0: `(k * 4 + tx/2) % 32`. For tx=0..7: tx=0: bank = `k*4 % 32` tx=1: bank = `k*4 % 32` tx=2: bank = `(k*4 + 1) % 32` tx=3: bank = `(k*4 + 1) % 32` ... That's 2-way conflict. With pad=1: `(k * 9 + tx) / 2 % 32 = (k*4 + k/2 + tx/2) % 32`. Not clean. To be safe, let me just not worry about shared memory bank conflicts too much and write the kernel. So threads in the same warp have m values `m_block + 0, m_block + 1, ..., m_block + 15`. They load A[m+tx, k] where k starts at 0 and increments. A[m+tx, k] = A[(m+tx) * K + k]. For a fixed k and varying m+tx in steps of 1: addresses are (m+0)*K+k, (m+1)*K+k, (m+2)*K+k, ..., (m+15)*K+k. With K=1024, these are 1024 elements apart! That's 2048 bytes (for half precision). A warp loads 16 elements spaced 2048 bytes apart. This is terrible coalescing - effectively 16 separate cache lines per load. This uses massive bandwidth. In contrast, my shared memory approach loads A[m, k] for a single m, with k varying. For a warp, consecutive threads load consecutive k values: A[m, k+0], A[m, k+1], ..., A[m, k+15]. These are consecutive in memory = perfectly coalesced! This is a huge difference. The naive kernel had terrible memory access patterns. The new shared memory kernel should be much faster. Similarly for B: in the naive kernel, threads in a warp have the same k but different n values (from indices). These are uncoalesced. In the new kernel, threads in a warp also have different n values, so B loads are still uncoalesced. But at least A loads are now optimal. The new kernel uses 248 blocks, each loading one row of A perfectly coalesced. Then each thread does K=1024 iterations reading A_shared[k] (fast) and B[k*N + n] (random). The B reads are uncoalesced but cached. Since B is only 2MB total, and we access it row by row, with K=1024 iterations per thread and 240 threads per block, each block touches 240*1024 = 245,760 elements of B. At 2 bytes each, that's 480KB of B reads per block. But due to caching, actual DRAM reads are much less. Total blocks: 248. Total B reads if no cache: 248 * 480KB = 119MB. At 3.35 TB/s, this is 0.035ms. With cache, much less. So the new kernel should be very fast. Let me also try increasing parallelism: block size = 128 instead of 256, so grid.y = 2. Total blocks = 496. More blocks might help with occupancy and reduce block idle time (currently 16 threads out of 256 are idle). With block size 128: - grid(M=248, 2) - Total blocks: 496 - Threads per block: 128 - Each thread does same amount of work - A load: each block loads A_shared via 128 threads, 8 loads per thread - More parallelism! One more optimization: load A in a vectorized way. For K=1024 and blockDim=128, each thread loads 8 elements. We can use `__half2` to load 2 at a time. ```cuda for (int k = threadIdx.x; k < K; k += blockDim.x) { A_shared[k] = A[m * K + k]; } ``` With K=1024 and blockDim=128, k takes values 0,1,2,...,127 for the first iteration, then 128,...,255, etc. Consecutive threads load consecutive addresses. Perfect coalescing. For even better performance, we could use `__half2` loads if the addresses are aligned. `A[m * K]` is aligned if m*K is even. K=1024 is even, so m*K is always even for integer m. The starting address is half-aligned. Actually, for `__half2` we need 4-byte alignment. `m * K * 2` bytes. K=1024, so m*1024*2 = m*2048. This is divisible by 4 for any integer m. So we can use `__half2` loads! Let me try to vectorize: ```cuda const __half2* A_vec = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(A + m * K); __half2* A_shared_vec = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(A_shared); for (int k = threadIdx.x; k < K / 2; k += blockDim.x) { A_shared_vec[k] = A_vec[k]; } ``` This loads 2 halfs at a time, reducing the number of memory instructions by 2x. For the computation loop, we still need to access individual halfs. We could process in pairs: ```cuda for (int k = 0; k < K; k += 2) { __half2 a_pair = *reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(&A_shared[k]); __half2 b_pair; b_pair.x = B[k * N + n]; b_pair.y = B[(k+1) * N + n]; accum += __half2float(a_pair.x) * __half2float(b_pair.x); accum += __half2float(a_pair.y) * __half2float(b_pair.y); } ``` But this is getting complex. And the B loads are scattered, so we can't vectorize them easily. For simplicity and correctness, let me stick to scalar loads for now. Actually, I just realized: K=1024. Let me finalize the code. ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline M = 248 N = 1024 K = 1024 INDEX_SIZE = 240 ALPHA = 1.0 BETA = 0.0 cpp_sources = r''' torch::Tensor gather_scatter_gemm( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, torch::Tensor indices, float alpha, float beta); ''' cuda_source = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> __global__ void gather_scatter_gemm_kernel( const at::Half* __restrict__ A, const at::Half* __restrict__ B, const at::Half* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, const int* __restrict__ indices, int M, int N, int K, int index_size, float alpha, float beta ) { __shared__ at::Half A_shared[1024]; int m = blockIdx.x; int idx_n = blockIdx.y * blockDim.x + threadIdx.x; for (int k = threadIdx.x; k < K; k += blockDim.x) { A_shared[k] = A[m * K + k]; } __syncthreads(); if (idx_n >= index_size) return; if (beta != 0.0f) { result += beta * static_cast<float>(C[m * N + n]); } D[m * N + n] = result; } torch::Tensor gather_scatter_gemm( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, torch::Tensor indices, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); int index_size = indices.size(0); auto D = torch::zeros({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device())); dim3 block(128); dim3 grid(M, (index_size + 127) / 128); gather_scatter_gemm_kernel<<<grid, block>>>( A.data_ptr<at::Half>(), B.data_ptr<at::Half>(), C.data_ptr<at::Half>(), D.data_ptr<float>(), indices.data_ptr<int>(), M, N, K, index_size, alpha, beta ); return D; } ''' gather_scatter_gemm_ext = load_inline( name="gather_scatter_gemm_optimized", cpp_sources=cpp_sources, cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=["gather_scatter_gemm"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math"], verbose=False, ) gather_scatter_gemm = gather_scatter_gemm_ext.gather_scatter_gemm class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, INDEX_SIZE, ALPHA, BETA): super().__init__() self.K = K self.N = N self.INDEX_SIZE = INDEX_SIZE self.alpha = ALPHA self.beta = BETA torch.manual_seed(42) self.register_buffer("B", torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device="cuda")) self.register_buffer("C", torch.randn(M, N, dtype=torch.float16, device="cuda")) self.register_buffer( "indices", torch.randperm(N, device="cuda")[:INDEX_SIZE].to(dtype=torch.int32), ) def forward(self, A): A = A.contiguous() B = self.B.contiguous() C = self.C.contiguous() indices = self.indices.contiguous() return gather_scatter_gemm(A, B, C, indices, self.alpha, self.beta) ``` Let me test this. For A with dimensions M x K in column-major, `A.at({i, k}) = A[k * M + i]`. In PyTorch, tensors are row-major by default. So `A[i, k] = A[i * K + k]`. In the actual memory: - For column-major A: `A[i, k]` -> memory offset `k * M + i` - For column-major B: `B[k, b_c_d_col]` -> memory offset `b_c_d_col * K + k` In PyTorch row-major: - `A[i, k]` -> memory offset `i * K + k` - `B[k, b_c_d_col]` -> memory offset `k * N + b_c_d_col` For the GEMM computation, we need: `D[i, j] = sum_k A[i, k] * B[k, j]` This is the same mathematical formula regardless of memory layout! The difference is only in how you compute the memory offset. In the CUTLASS reference, the pseudocode computes logical `sum_k A[i, k] * B[k, j]` and stores in `D[i, j]`. The memory layout affects the internal storage offsets but not the mathematical formula. If the reference does `A = torch.randn(K, M).t().cuda()` then `A` has shape (M, K) but stride (1, M), making it column-major in Fortran terms. If the reference uses `torch.randn(K, M).t()` for A, then A is logically M x K with column-major strides. And `A[i, k]` accesses memory at offset `i + k * M`. But `A[i, k]` in PyTorch uses strides, so it computes `i * stride[0] + k * stride[1]`. For `torch.randn(K, M).t()`, strides are (1, M). So `A[i, k]` -> `i * 1 + k * M`. If the reference B is `torch.randn(N, K).t()` (shape K x N, strides (1, N)), then `B[k, j]` -> `k + j * N`. This would be the column-major access pattern! For column-major A with shape (M, K): In PyTorch, this is equivalent to a row-major tensor of shape (K, M) that has been transposed. Or just a tensor with shape (M, K) and stride (1, M). Similarly, B with shape (K, N) and stride (1, N) in column-major. So the reference might do: ```python A = torch.randn(K, M, dtype=torch.float16).t().cuda() # shape (M, K), stride (1, M) B = torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16).t().cuda() # shape (K, N), stride (1, N) C = torch.randn(N, M, dtype=torch.float32).t().cuda() # shape (M, N), stride (1, M) ``` And D initialized as zeros with shape (M, N) and stride (1, M). Then the GEMM computation would be: ```python for i in range(M): for j in range(index_size): n = indices[j] accum = 0 for k in range(K): accum += float(A[i, k]) * float(B[k, n]) D[i, n] = alpha * accum + beta * C[i, n] ``` Because PyTorch handles the strides automatically, `A[i, k]` accesses `A.storage()[i + k * M]` and `B[k, n]` accesses `B.storage()[k + n * K]`. But my CUDA kernel uses raw pointers: ```cuda A[m * K + k] // assumes stride[0] = K, stride[1] = 1 B[k * N + n] // assumes stride[0] = N, stride[1] = 1 ``` If the reference uses column-major strides, my raw pointer indexing is WRONG.
